Variance estimation for Sequential Monte Carlo algorithms: a backward sampling approach
Yazid Janati El Idrissi, Sylvain Le Corff, Yohan Petetin

Abstract
. In this paper, we consider the problem of online asymptotic variance estimation for particle filtering and smoothing. Current solutions for the particle filter rely on the particle genealogy and are either unstable or hard to tune in practice. We propose to mitigate these limitations by introducing a new estimator of the asymptotic variance based on the so called backward weights. The resulting estimator is weakly consistent and trades computational cost for more stability and reduced variance. We also propose a more computationally efficient estimator inspired by the PaRIS algorithm of [ 33 ] . As an application, particle smoothing is considered and an estimator of the asymptotic variance of the Forward Filtering Backward Smoothing estimator applied to additive functionals is provided.



1.
Introduction

Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) methods offer a flexible framework for the approximation of posterior distributions in the context of Bayesian inference, for instance in
Hidden Markov Models
(HMM). These models presuppose that the observations are defined using an unobserved process assumed to be a Markov chain. In such a setting, we are particularly interested in estimating the law of a hidden state given all past observations referred to as the filtering distribution and the laws of sequences of states given past and future observations, referred to as smoothing distributions. These distributions can be approximated by weighted empirical measures associated with random samples, usually known as
particles
. All SMC methods are based on successive importance sampling and resampling steps. When a new observation is available, new particles are sampled according to an importance distribution and then they are
weighted to match the target distribution. Finally, through a resampling scheme, particles with large weights are duplicated while low weighted particles are discarded.
This general procedure has been used in a wide range of applications such as signal processing, target tracking, econometrics, biology, see
[
3
,
16
,
6
]
and the references therein.

The quantification of the Monte Carlo error of SMC estimators is a major challenge. For a variety of SMC methods such as the
bootstrap filter

[
23
]
or the
Forward Filtering Backward Smoothing
(FFBS)
[
37
]
algorithms,
Central Limit Theorems
(CLT) with theoretical expressions of the asymptotic variance (in the number of particles) have been derived
[
13
,
5
,
26
,
15
]
. However, these expressions are not computable in practice and give rise to the natural question of their estimation.
Since this problem appears in an online context, a critical constraint is that the samples
produced by the original SMC algorithm should be recycled to compute such estimates.

This problem has received some satisfactory solutions recently: a simple estimator
of the asymptotic variance when multinomial resampling is used has
been proposed in
[
4
]

for the bootstrap filter algorithm
and has since been refined in
[
28
,
32
,
18
]
.
The computation of the associated asymptotic variance estimator at time
t
is based on tracing the genealogy of each particle down to time
0
. Although it has been shown to be consistent as the number of particles
N
grows to infinity,
it is particularly prone to instability when
t
is large, as the successive resampling steps lead to the well known path degeneracy issue.
After a few time steps, particles are likely to share the same ancestor at time
0
which in turn makes the asymptotic variance estimates collapse. To overcome this degeneracy issue,
[
32
]
proposed to only trace a part of the genealogy of each particle according to a fixed-lag parameter following the fixed-lag smoothing approach introduced in
[
31
]
. As long as the number of particles is balanced with the chosen lag, the bias introduced by considering only the most recent ancestors of each particle can be controlled as shown in
[
32
]
. However, while this alternative estimator remains easily computable,
choosing an optimal lag is a non trivial task which makes this approach hard to tune in practice. We thus address the limitations of current asymptotic variance estimators in this paper.

The first contribution of this paper is to propose a parameter free estimator of the asymptotic variance associated with the bootstrap particle filter with multinomial resampling that trades computational cost for stability and reduced variance. The construction of our
estimator starts from the observation that
the aforementioned degeneracy
is similar to that of classical SMC-based smoothing algorithms
[
17
,
20
,
35
]
or Particle Markov Chain Monte Carlo algorithms such as the
Particle Gibbs sampler
[
1
]
.
In both cases, a backward sampling step which aims at diversifying the particle trajectories has shown to be a reliable workaround that decreases the (theoretical) variance of the estimators at the expense of higher computational cost
[
22
,
15
,
33
,
11
,
29
,
7
]
.
We thus aim at introducing such a mechanism in the estimation of the asymptotic variance.
The construction of our estimator
relies on the analysis conducted in
[
28
]
in which it is shown that the estimator of
[
4
]
can be interpreted as a conditional expectation
with respect to the indices that retrace the genealogy of the particles,
given all the particles and ancestors.
We show that this construction still holds when the distribution of the indices relies on the backward importance weights. The resulting estimator is computed by averaging
auxiliary statistics that are very similar to those of the
forward implementation
of the FFBS for additive functionals
[
12
]
and can be thus updated online. The time complexity per update of our estimator is of order
N^{3}
. Driven by the efficient implementation of the FFBS for additive functionals developed in
[
33
]
, we show that the computational cost of our estimator can be reduced from
\mathcal{O}(N^{3})
to
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
by means of additional Monte Carlo simulation while remaining as competitive in terms of bias and variance.

We next focus on the FFBS algorithm for the estimation of smoothing estimators.
Despite the fact that a CLT has been obtained for estimators based on the FFBS, no variance estimator has been proposed in the literature. We show that our previous construction enables us to
fill this gap and we thus provide
a consistent estimator in the case of additive functionals which are
particularly critical, for instance in the Expectation Maximization framework. Again, this estimator
can be computed online and in the
particular case of
marginal smoothing
its computational cost can be drastically
reduced.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
3
we briefly review the SMC framework and discuss the current estimators of the asymptotic variance proposed in
[
4
,
28
,
32
]
. In Section
4
, we introduce
our estimator based on the backward weights, propose an online implementation and establish its asymptotic properties. In Section
5
, we extend our derivations to the FFBS algorithm and provide a consistent asymptotic variance estimator. We finally
validate our results with numerical experiments in Section
6
. Notably, we show empirically that our novel estimator for the filter has a favourable dependence on the time horizon
t
in comparison with the existing estimators. All additional proofs and discussions can be found in the appendix.

2.
Notations

For any measurable space
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
, we denote by
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{E})
the set of
\mathbb{R}
valued,
\mathcal{E}
-measurable functions, by
\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{E})
the subset of
\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{E})
of bounded functions on
\mathsf{E}
, and by
\mathbb{M}(\mathsf{E})
the set of measures on
\mathsf{E}
. For any
\mu\in\mathbb{M}(\mathrm{E})
and
h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{E})
, we write

\mu(h):=\int_{\mathsf{E}}h(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x).

For any transition kernel
M
from
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
to another measurable space
(\mathsf{G},\mathcal{G})
, define

M[h](x):=\int_{\mathsf{G}}M(x,\mathrm{d}y)h(y),\quad\forall h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{G}),\quad\forall x\in\mathsf{E}.

and write
\mu M
the measure defined on
\mathsf{G}
by

\mu M(A):=\int_{\mathsf{E}}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)M(x,A),\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{G}.

If
M_{1}
is a transition kernel from
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
to
(\mathsf{G},\mathcal{G})
and
M_{2}
a transition kernel from
(\mathsf{G},\mathcal{G})
to a measurable space
(\mathsf{H},\mathcal{H})
, then
M_{1}M_{2}
is the transition kernel from
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
to
(\mathsf{H},\mathcal{H})
defined by

M_{1}M_{2}(x,A)=\int_{\mathsf{G}}M_{1}(x,\mathrm{d}y)M_{2}(y,A),\quad\forall x\in\mathsf{E},\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{H}.

In addition,
M_{1}\otimes M_{2}
is the transition kernel from
(\mathsf{E},\mathcal{E})
to
(\mathsf{G}\times\mathsf{H},\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{H})
defined by

M_{1}\otimes M_{2}(x,A):=\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}(y,z)M_{1}(x,\mathrm{d}y)M_{2}(y,\mathrm{d}z),\quad\forall x\in\mathsf{E},\quad A\in\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{H}.

In particular, for all
N\geq 1
,we will write
M^{\otimes N}
for
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}M
. For two
\mathcal{E}
-measurable functions
f,g
the tensor product is defined as

f\otimes g:\mathsf{E}^{2}\ni(x,y)\mapsto f(x)g(y).

The sets
\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{N}^{*}
are respectively the sets of natural numbers and positive natural numbers. The
\mathbf{L}_{q}
norm of a random variable
X
is
\|X\|_{q}:=\mathbb{E}\big[|X|^{q}\big]^{1/q}
. The supremum norm of
f\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathsf{E})
is denoted by
|f|_{\infty}
. The unit function
\mathbf{1}
is such that
\mathbf{1}(x)=1
for all
x\in\mathsf{E}
.
The transpose of a matrix
A
is denoted
A^{\top}
. If
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
are two matrices, then the Hadamard product
A\odot B
is the element-wise product, i.e for all
1\leq i\leq M
and
1\leq j\leq N
,
(A\odot B)_{i,j}=a_{i,j}b_{i,j}
.
If
A\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
, then
\mathrm{Diag}(A)
is the
N\times N
diagonal matrix such that for all
1\leq i\leq N
,
\mathrm{Diag}(A)_{i,i}=A_{i,i}
and if
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 1}
then
\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{x})
is the
N\times N
diagonal matrix such that
\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{x})_{i,i}=\bm{x}_{i}
. For
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
[a:b]:=\mathbb{N}\cap[a,b]
and
[b]:=[1:b]
. If
f
is a mapping from
[N]^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\bm{f}
the associated
N\times N
matrix such that
\bm{f}_{i,j}=f(i,j)
. Finally, we adopt the following conventions. Given some set
\{\xi^{i}_{s}\}_{s\in[0:t],i\in[N]}
, we write
\xi^{1:N}_{t}:=(\xi^{1}_{t},\cdots,\xi^{N}_{t})
,
\xi^{k_{0:t}}_{0:t}:=(\xi^{k_{0}}_{0},\cdots,\xi^{k_{t}}_{t})
,
\bm{\xi}^{1:N}_{0:t}:=\big\{\xi^{k_{0:t}}_{0:t}\big\}_{k_{0:t}\in[N]^{t+1}}
.

3.
Sequential Monte Carlo

In this section, we first review the bootstrap particle filter methodology and recall the main asymptotic results associated
with the estimates produced by this algorithm. A state of the art and a discussion of the estimation of the asymptotic variances
related to this algorithm are also presented.

3.1.
Definitions

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
be a general measurable space. Let
\mathrm{M}_{0}
and
(\mathrm{M}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
be a probability measure on
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
and a sequence of Markov transition kernels on
\mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{X}
, respectively. Consider also a family
(g_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
of non-negative
\mathcal{X}
-measurable functions, referred to as potentials. Throughout this paper, we make the following assumptions on
\{\mathrm{M}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
.

(
A1
)

The probability measure
\mathrm{M}_{0}
admits
m_{0}
as probability density with respect to some reference measure
\nu\in\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{X})
. For all
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{t}\in\mathsf{X}
,
\mathrm{M}_{t+1}(x_{t},.)
admits
m_{t+1}(x_{t},.)
as probability density with respect to
\nu
.

(
A2
)

There exists a constant
G_{\infty}>0
such that for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathsf{X}
,
0<g_{t}(x)\leq G_{\infty}.

Define the sequence of unnormalized transition kernels
(\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{t+1})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
where, for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{t}\in\mathsf{X}
and
A\in\mathcal{X}
,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{t+1}(x_{t},A):=g_{t}(x_{t})\mathrm{M}_{t+1}(x_{t},A),

and, for any
s,t\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,

\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{s:t}:=\begin{cases}\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{s}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}&\text{ if }s\leq t,\\
\text{ Id }&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s:t}
denote its marginal with respect to the variable
x_{t}
, i.e. for all
x_{s-1}\in\mathsf{X}
and all measurable set
A
,

\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s:t}(x_{s-1},A):=\int_{\mathsf{X}^{t-s+1}}\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{s:t}(x_{s-1},\mathrm{d}x_{s},\dotsc,\mathrm{d}x_{t})\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x_{t}).

Define recursively the sequence of measures
(\gamma_{0:t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
by

\gamma_{0}(\mathrm{d}x_{0}):=\mathrm{M}_{0}(\mathrm{d}x_{0}),\quad\gamma_{0:t}(\mathrm{d}x_{0:t}):=\gamma_{0:t-1}(\mathrm{d}x_{0:t-1})\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}(x_{t-1},\mathrm{d}x_{t}),

(3.1)

and let
\gamma_{t}(A)=\int_{\mathsf{X}^{t+1}}\gamma_{0:t}(\mathrm{d}x_{0:t})\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x_{t})
for all measurable set
A
. Sequential Monte Carlo algorithms aim at solving recursively the filtering problem, i.e. at estimating the sequence of probability measures defined as

\eta_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t}):=\gamma_{t}^{-1}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t}),\quad\phi_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t}):=g_{t}(x_{t})\eta_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t})/\eta_{t}(g_{t}),

(3.2)

respectively called the
predictive
and
filtering
measures. Note that
\eta_{t}
can be computed recursively using

\eta_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t})=\int\phi_{t-1}(\mathrm{d}x_{t-1})\mathrm{M}_{t}(x_{t-1},\mathrm{d}x_{t}).

(3.3)

We motivate these definitions with the following example.

Example 3.1
.

Hidden Markov models
consist of an unobserved state process
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and observations
\{Y_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
.
They respectively evolve in two general measurable spaces
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
and
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
.
It is assumed that
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Markov chain with transition kernels
(\mathrm{M}_{t+1})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
initial distribution
\mathrm{M}_{0}
. Given the states
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
, the observations
\{Y_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent
and for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the conditional distribution of the observation
Y_{t}
only depends on the current state
X_{t}
. This distribution is written
G_{t}(X_{t},.)
and admits the potential
g_{t}(x_{t},.)
as density (the dependency in
Y_{t}
is made implicit and we drop the second argument). Given an observation record
Y_{0:t}
, the predictive and filtering
distributions (
3.2
) are then the distributions of
X_{t}
given
Y_{0:t-1}
and
X_{t}
given
Y_{0:t}
respectively.

These two distributions are of considerable interest in Bayesian filtering as they enable the estimation of the hidden states through the observed data record. Unfortunately only in a few cases, such as discrete state spaces or linear and Gaussian HMM, can they be obtained in closed form, see
[
3
,
6
]
for a complete overview.

3.2.
Particle filter

We now illustrate how to obtain empirical estimates of
\eta_{t}
and
\phi_{t}
in an online manner through Monte Carlo simulation.
Assume that at time
t
the empirical measure
\eta_{t}^{N}(\mathrm{d}x_{t}):=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\xi^{i}_{t}}(\mathrm{d}x_{t})
based on random samples
\{\xi^{i}_{t}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
approximates
\eta_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t})
. Plugging
\eta_{t}^{N}
in (
3.2
) provides an approximation of
\phi_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t})
,

\phi_{t}^{N}(\mathrm{d}x_{t}):=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{W}^{i}_{t}\delta_{\xi^{i}_{t}}(\mathrm{d}x_{t}),

where

\mathcal{W}^{i}_{t}:=\Omega^{-1}_{t}\omega^{i}_{t}
,
\omega^{i}_{t}:=g_{t}(\xi^{i}_{t})
and
\Omega_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\omega^{i}_{t}.

Replacing
\phi_{t}
by
\phi_{t}^{N}
in (
3.3
), we obtain the mixture
\phi_{t}^{N}\mathrm{M}_{t+1}
which allows to construct
\eta_{t+1}^{N}
by drawing
N
samples from it. We first sample for all
1\leq i\leq N
an ancestor index
A^{i}_{t}\sim\mathrm{Categorical}(\mathcal{W}^{1:N}_{t})
, and then sample
\xi^{i}_{t+1}\sim\mathrm{M}_{t+1}(\xi^{A^{i}_{t}}_{t},\cdot)
. The algorithm is initialized with the approximation of
\eta_{0}=\mathrm{M}_{0}
,
\eta^{N}_{0}:=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\xi^{i}_{0}}(\mathrm{d}x_{t})
where
\xi^{1:N}_{0}\sim\mathrm{M}_{0}^{\otimes N}
and coincides with the
bootstrap
algorithm with multinomial resampling
[
23
]
.
Note that this mechanism has been extended in many directions in the past decades
[
34
,
14
,
6
]
.

Alongside
\eta_{t}^{N}
and
\phi_{t}^{N}
, the particle approximation of the unnormalized marginal
\gamma_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t})
is given by

\gamma_{t}^{N}(\mathrm{d}x_{t}):=\bigg\{\prod_{s=0}^{t-1}N^{-1}\Omega_{s}\bigg\}\eta_{t}^{N}(\mathrm{d}x_{t}),\quad\forall t>0,

(3.4)

and
\gamma_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}x_{0})=\eta_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}x_{0}).
In particular,
\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)
is unbiased for any measurable function
h

[
9
]
.

In the remainder of this paper, we denote by
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}
the
\sigma
-field containing all the particles and ancestors up to time
t
, i.e.
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}:=\sigma\left(\bm{\xi}^{1:N}_{0:t},\bm{A}^{1:N}_{0:t-1}\right)
.

3.3.
Asymptotic variance estimation in particle filters

The particle filter described above yields consistent estimators, see for instance
[
3
,
16
,
6
,
30
,
9
]

and references therein for a complete overview. Indeed, for a test function
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X})

and under assumption

(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:B})}}
, the SMC estimators satisfy a Strong Law of Large
Numbers when the number of particles
N
goes to infinity, i.e.

\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)\overset{a.s.}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\gamma_{t}(h),\quad\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\overset{a.s.}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\eta_{t}(h),\quad\phi_{t}^{N}(h)\overset{a.s.}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\phi_{t}(h).

(3.5)

Under the same assumptions, CLTs for
\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)
,
\eta_{t}^{N}(h)
and
\phi_{t}^{N}(h)
are also available
[
13
,
5
]
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sqrt{N}\big(\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)-\gamma_{t}(h)\big)&\underset{N\to\infty}{\Longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\big(0,\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\gamma,t}(h)\big),\\
\sqrt{N}\big(\eta_{t}^{N}(h)-\eta_{t}(h)\big)&\underset{N\to\infty}{\Longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\big(0,\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\eta,t}\left(h\right)\big),\\
\sqrt{N}\big(\phi_{t}^{N}(h)-\phi_{t}(h)\big)&\underset{N\to\infty}{\Longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\big(0,\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\phi,t}(h)\big),\end{cases}

(3.6)

where
\Longrightarrow
denotes weak convergence and

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\gamma,t}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\big\{\gamma_{s}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{s}\big(\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s+1:t}[h]^{2}\big)-\gamma_{t}(h)^{2}\big\},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\eta,t}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\frac{\gamma_{s}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{s}\big(\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s+1:t}[h-\eta_{t}(h)]^{2}\big)}{\gamma_{t}(\mathbf{1})^{2}},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\phi,t}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\frac{\gamma_{s}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{s}\big(\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s+1:t}[g_{t}\{h-\phi_{t}(h)\}]^{2}\big)}{\gamma_{t+1}(\mathbf{1})^{2}}.

(3.9)

An intuitive derivation of (
3.7
) is proposed in Section
D
of the appendix.
The authors of
[
4
]
propose to estimate (
3.7
) online using the samples produced by the particle filter described above.
Their estimator is based on the genealogy of the particle system induced by the successive resampling steps
of the particle filter.
From the indices
A^{i}_{t}
,
it is possible to trace back the ancestors of each particle and deduce the corresponding ancestor at time
t=0
.
More interestingly, these ancestors
can be computed in a forward way
by introducing the Eve indices
\mathrm{E}^{i}_{t,0}
. For all
i\in[1:N]
,
\mathrm{E}^{i}_{t,0}
describes the index of the ancestor at time
0
of particle
\xi^{i}_{t}
and can be
computed from

\mathrm{E}^{i}_{t,0}=\mathrm{E}^{A^{i}_{t-1}}_{t-1,0}\mathbbm{1}_{t>0}+i\mathbbm{1}_{t=0}.

(3.10)

The asymptotic variance estimator of
\eta_{t}^{N}(h)
obtained in
[
4
]
reads (see Section
B.1
of the supplementary for a proof):

\mathcal{V}^{N}_{\eta,t}(h):=-N^{-1}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{E}^{i}_{t,0}\neq\mathrm{E}^{j}_{t,0}}\big\{h(\xi^{i}_{t})-\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\big\}\big\{h(\xi^{j}_{t})-\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\big\}.

(3.11)

We sometimes refer to
\mathcal{V}^{N}_{\eta,t}(h)

as the CLE (Chan & Lai Estimator).
Note that it can be computed online in a remarkably simple way since the Eve indices (
3.10
) are computed recursively. However, the counterpart of its computational simplicity is that it degenerates as soon as the ancestral paths coalesce. Indeed, it is widely known in the SMC
literature that all lineages eventually end up with the same ancestor when
t
is large enough with respect to the number of samples
N
(see e.g.
[
20
, Section 2.2]
for a more detailed explanation).
This means that for a fixed
N
, as
t
grows and
s\ll t
,
E^{i}_{s,0}=E^{j}_{s,0}
for all
(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
\mathcal{V}^{N}_{\eta,t}(h)=0
for any test function
h
.

The degeneracy problem concerning (
3.11
) is partially addressed in
[
32
]
by truncating the genealogy of the particle system. Denoting
\lambda\in[t]
the lag and
\mathrm{E}^{i}_{t,t-\lambda}
the ancestor of
\xi^{i}_{t}
at time
t-\lambda
, their estimator reads

\mathcal{V}^{N,\lambda}_{\eta,t}(h):=-N^{-1}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\lx@nobreakspace\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{E}^{i}_{t,t-\lambda}\neq\mathrm{E}^{j}_{t,t-\lambda}}\big\{h(\xi^{i}_{t})-\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\big\}\big\{h(\xi^{j}_{t})-\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\big\}.

(3.12)

In the regime where (
3.11
) degenerates, (
3.12
) can be made stable provided that the lag
\lambda
is chosen such that there is little asymptotic bias. However, besides the strong mixing case for which the authors propose a heuristic, the practical choice of such a
\lambda
, although crucial, is a non trivial task.

In
[
28
]
, the CLE is revisited using different techniques based on
[
8
,
1
,
2
]
. These tools enable them to derive a weakly consistent term
by term estimator of (
3.7
) based on
the unbiased estimation of
\gamma_{t}(h)^{2}
and of each
\gamma_{s}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{s}\big(\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s+1:t}[h]^{2}\big)
,
for all
s\in[0:t]
.
The construction of this second estimator is appealing and insightful in that it helps identifying the deep root of the degeneracy in the CLE. They indeed show that (
3.11
) and each
\gamma_{s}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{s}\big(\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s+1:t}[h]^{2}\big)
can be interpreted as a conditional expectation with respect to particle indices that retrace the ancestral paths.
Indeed, by introducing discrete
random variables
K^{1}_{0:t}
and

K^{2}_{0:t}
such that conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}
,
K^{1}_{t}
and
K^{2}_{t}
are distributed uniformly on
[N]
and such that for any
s\in[0:t-1]
,

K^{1}_{s}=A^{K^{1}_{s+1}}_{s},\quad K^{2}_{s}=\mathbbm{1}_{K^{1}_{s+1}\neq K^{2}_{s+1}}A^{K^{2}_{s+1}}_{s}+\mathbbm{1}_{K^{1}_{s+1}=K^{2}_{s+1}}C_{s},

where
C_{s}\sim\mathrm{Categorical}(\mathcal{W}^{1:N}_{s})
, then for example

\mathcal{V}^{N}_{\eta,t}(h)=-N\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{s=0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{K^{1}_{s}\neq K^{2}_{s}}\{h(\xi^{K^{1}_{t}}_{t})-\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\}\{h(\xi^{K^{2}_{t}}_{t})-\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\}\bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right].

An intuitive extension is to replace the deterministic assignments
K^{1}_{s}=A^{K^{1}_{s+1}}_{s}
and
K^{2}_{s}=A^{K^{2}_{s+1}}_{s}
by random ones based on backward sampling. Essentially, the backward kernel
samples at time
s
an index
i
with probability proportional to
\omega^{i}_{s}m_{s+1}(\xi^{i}_{s},\xi^{K^{1}_{s+1}}_{s+1})
(resp.
\omega^{i}_{s}m_{s+1}(\xi^{i}_{s},\xi^{K^{2}_{s+1}}_{s+1})
) and thus allows to consider relevant trajectories which are not necessarily ancestral trajectories.

4.
Variance estimation with backward sampling

In this section we present three variance estimators for the bootstrap particle filter. In Section
4.1
, we lay out our methodology and derive a term by term variance estimator; its computation
is detailed
in Section
4.2
. In Sections
4.3
and
4.4
, we provide two additional estimators that have a lower computational cost.
All estimators and justifications are provided for the distribution (
3.4
). We give the expressions for the variance estimators of the predictor and filter and provide their justification in Section
B.2
of the appendix.

4.1.
Term by term variance estimator

For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{B}_{t}:=\{0,1\}^{t+1}
. Denote by
\mathbf{0}
the null vector in
\mathcal{B}_{t}
and
e_{s}
the vector
with 1 at position
s
and
0
elsewhere.
Let
(X_{s},X^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in[0:t]}
be a bivariate Markov chain in
(\mathsf{X}^{2},\mathcal{X}^{\otimes 2})
and depending on
b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}
with initial distribution
\mathcal{M}^{b_{0}}_{0}
and transition kernels
\mathcal{M}^{b_{t}}_{t}
,
t\geq 1
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{b_{0}}_{0}(\mathrm{d}x_{0},\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{M}_{0}(\mathrm{d}x_{0})\{\mathbbm{1}_{b_{0}=0}\mathrm{M}_{0}(\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{0})+\mathbbm{1}_{b_{0}=1}\delta_{x_{0}}(\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{0})\},

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{b_{t}}_{t}(x,x^{\prime};\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{M}_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}z)\{\mathbbm{1}_{b_{t}=0}\mathrm{M}_{t}(x^{\prime},\mathrm{d}z^{\prime})+\mathbbm{1}_{b_{t}=1}\delta_{z}(\mathrm{d}z^{\prime})\},\quad\forall t\geq 1.

Define also for any
b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}
the measure
\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}
by
\mathcal{Q}_{b,0}(\mathrm{d}x_{0},\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{0})=\mathcal{M}^{b_{0}}_{0}(\mathrm{d}x_{0},\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{0})
and for
t\geq 1
:

\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}\big(\mathrm{d}x_{0:t},\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{0:t}\big):=\mathcal{M}^{b_{0}}_{0}\big(\mathrm{d}x_{0},\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{0}\big)\prod_{s=0}^{t-1}g_{s}^{\otimes 2}(x_{s},x^{\prime}_{s})\prod_{s=1}^{t}\mathcal{M}^{b_{s}}_{s}\big(x_{s-1},x^{\prime}_{s-1};\mathrm{d}x_{s},\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{s}\big).

(4.2)

The measure
\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}
is the joint distribution (
3.1
) of the Feynman-Kac model defined by the initial distribution
\mathcal{M}^{b_{0}}_{0}
, the transition kernels
\{\mathcal{M}^{b_{s}}_{s}\}_{s\in[1:t]}
and by the potential functions
\{g_{s}^{\otimes 2}\}_{s\in[0:t-1]}
.
Remark that for any
h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X})
, writing
h_{t}:x_{0:t}\mapsto h(x_{t})
, we have that

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})=\gamma_{t}(h)^{2}
and

\mathcal{Q}_{e_{s},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})=\gamma_{s}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{s}(\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s+1:t}[h]^{2}).

(4.3)

A generalization of (
4.3
) is proved in Proposition
5.1
. Consequently, for
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X})

\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\gamma,t}(h)
in (
3.7
) can be rewritten as

\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\gamma,t}(h)=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\big\{\mathcal{Q}_{e_{s},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})-\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})\big\},

(4.4)

where
h_{t}:x_{0:t}\mapsto h(x_{t})
.
Following this observation,
for a given
b
,
an estimator of
\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}(h_{t})
could be obtained with a single run of a
bootstrap particle filter in augmented dimension (i.e. relying on the
the bivariate transition
\mathcal{M}^{b_{t}}_{t}
at time
t
and on the weighing of the associated particles with
g^{\otimes 2}_{t}
). As a direct
extension of (
3.4
),
this estimator would be unbiased and
as a byproduct, we would get an unbiased estimator of (
4.4
).
However, this procedure is not in line with our initial objective in the sense
that we aim at estimating (
4.4
) with the particles and indices already available.

In order to motivate and introduce our approach, let us consider the static situation where
t=0
,
b_{0}=0
and let
(h,f)\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X})^{2}
. Then,

\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{i\neq j}h(\xi^{i}_{0})f(\xi^{j}_{0}),\quad\xi^{1:N}_{0}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathrm{M}_{0}

(4.5)

is an unbiased and almost sure convergent estimator of
\mathcal{Q}_{0,0}(h\otimes f)=\mathrm{M}_{0}^{\otimes 2}(h\otimes f)
and only relies on i.i.d. samples from
\mathrm{M}_{0}
rather than
\mathrm{M}_{0}^{\otimes 2}
. Note that for
h=f
, we thus get an unbiased estimator of
\gamma_{0}(h)^{2}
. If
b_{0}=1
, then
N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}h(\xi^{i}_{0})f(\xi^{i}_{0})
is an unbiased and consistent estimator of
\mathcal{Q}_{1,0}(h\otimes f)=\mathrm{M}_{0}(hf)
.

Taking advantage of the fact that the particles at time
t
are i.i.d. conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t-1}
as we use multinomial resampling for the particle filter, we can carry these simple observations to the sequential case in two directions as we now detail. Define for any
t\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
the functional version of the backward weights:

\beta^{N}_{t}(x,y):=\frac{g_{t-1}(y)m_{t}(y,x)}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\omega^{\ell}_{t-1}m_{t}(\xi^{\ell}_{t-1},x)},\quad\forall(x,y)\in\mathsf{X}^{2}.

(4.6)

Assume that
t=1
and define for any
(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\in[N]^{2}
the following random variables that involve the backward weights

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{0}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Omega_{0}^{2}}{N(N-1)}\sum_{k^{1:2}_{1}\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{1}\neq k^{2}_{1}}\beta^{N}_{1}(\xi^{k^{1}_{1}}_{1},\xi^{k^{1}_{0}}_{0})\beta^{N}_{1}(\xi^{k^{2}_{1}}_{1},\xi^{k^{2}_{0}}_{0})h(\xi^{k^{1}_{1}}_{1})f(\xi^{k^{2}_{1}}_{1}),

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{1}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Omega_{0}^{2}}{N}\sum_{k^{1:2}_{1}\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{1}=k^{2}_{1}}\beta^{N}_{1}(\xi^{k^{1}_{1}}_{1},\xi^{k^{1}_{0}}_{0})\mathcal{W}^{k^{2}_{0}}_{0}h(\xi^{k^{1}_{1}}_{1})f(\xi^{k^{2}_{1}}_{1}),

(4.8)

and also the following which involve the ancestors

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{GT}}_{0}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Omega_{0}^{2}}{N(N-1)}\sum_{k^{1:2}_{1}\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{A^{k^{1}_{1}}_{0}=k^{1}_{0},A^{k^{2}_{1}}_{0}=k^{2}_{0},k^{1}_{1}\neq k^{2}_{1}}h(\xi^{k^{1}_{1}}_{1})f(\xi^{k^{2}_{1}}_{1}),\lx@nobreakspace

(4.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{GT}}_{1}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Omega_{0}^{2}}{N}\sum_{k^{1:2}_{1}\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{0}=A^{k^{1}_{1}}_{0},k^{1}_{1}=k^{2}_{1}}\mathcal{W}^{k^{2}_{0}}_{0}h(\xi^{k^{1}_{1}}_{1})f(\xi^{k^{2}_{1}}_{1}),

(4.10)

Here,
\mathsf{BS}
and
\mathsf{GT}
correspond to backward sampling and genealogy tracing, respectively. Consider also Lemma
4.1
which states a crucial identity involving the backward weights.

Lemma 4.1
.

For all
t\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
(x,y)\in\mathsf{X}^{2}
,

\beta^{N}_{t}(x,y)\phi_{t-1}^{N}\mathrm{M}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{g_{t-1}(y)}{\Omega_{t-1}}\mathrm{M}_{t}(y,\mathrm{d}x).

(4.11)

and for any
(k_{t-1},k_{t})\in[N]^{2}
and
h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X})
,

\mathbb{E}\big[\beta^{N}_{t}(\xi^{k_{t}}_{t},\xi^{k_{t-1}}_{t-1})h(\xi^{k_{t}}_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t-1}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{k_{t-1}=A^{k_{t}}_{t-1}}h(\xi^{k_{t}}_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t-1}\big]=\mathcal{W}^{k_{t-1}}_{t-1}\mathrm{M}_{t}[h](\xi^{k_{t-1}}_{t-1}).

(4.12)

The proof is postponed to Section
A.2
in the appendix. Applying Lemma
4.1
to (
4.7
) and (
4.9
) and using that given
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{0}
the particles at
t=1
are i.i.d., we get

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{0}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{0}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{GT}}_{0}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{0}\big]=g^{\otimes 2}_{0}(\xi^{k^{1}_{0}}_{0},\xi^{k^{2}_{0}}_{0})\mathcal{M}^{0}_{1}[h\otimes f](\xi^{k^{1}_{0}}_{0},\xi^{k^{2}_{0}}_{0}),

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{k^{1:2}_{0}\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{0}\neq k^{2}_{0}}\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{0}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{k^{1:2}_{0}\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{0}\neq k^{2}_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{0}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{0}\big]\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{k^{1:2}_{0}\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{0}\neq k^{2}_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{GT}}_{0}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{0}\big]\bigg]

\displaystyle=N(N-1)\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{0},1}(h\otimes f).

Similarly,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{1}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{0}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{GT}}_{1}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{0}\big]=g^{\otimes 2}_{0}(\xi^{k^{1}_{0}}_{0},\xi^{k^{2}_{0}}_{0})\mathcal{M}^{1}_{1}[h\otimes f](\xi^{k^{1}_{0}}_{0},\xi^{k^{2}_{0}}_{0});

and

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{k^{1:2}_{0}\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{0}\neq k^{2}_{0}}\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{1}(k^{1}_{0},k^{2}_{0})\bigg]=N(N-1)\mathcal{Q}_{e_{1},1}(h\otimes f).

Therefore, it is possible to derive
unbiased estimators of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{0},1}(h\otimes f)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{e_{1},1}(h\otimes f)
(but also
\mathcal{Q}_{e_{0},1}(h\otimes f)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(1,1),1}(h\otimes f)
)
with a single run of the particle filter in two different ways: either by using the backward weights and (
4.7
)-(
4.8
), or by using directly the ancestry of the particles and (
4.9
)-(
4.10
). The asymptotic variance estimators proposed in
[
4
,
28
,
32
,
18
]
are all based on the latter solution, while in this paper we instead focus on estimators based on the backward weights.

We now generalize the derivations performed in the case
t=1
. Denote by
\Lambda_{1,t}
and
\Lambda_{2,t}
the discrete measures conditioned on
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}
and defined by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,t}(k_{0:t})

\displaystyle:=N^{-1}\prod_{s=1}^{t}\beta_{s}(k_{s},k_{s-1}),

(4.13)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2,t}(k^{1}_{0:t};k^{2}_{0:t})

\displaystyle:=N^{-1}\prod_{s=1}^{t}\left\{\mathbbm{1}_{k^{2}_{s}=k^{1}_{s}}\mathcal{W}^{k^{2}_{s-1}}_{s-1}+\mathbbm{1}_{k^{2}_{s}\neq k^{1}_{s}}\beta_{s}(k^{2}_{s},k^{2}_{s-1})\right\}.

(4.14)

Specific choices of kernels
\{\beta_{s}\}_{s=1}^{t}
are, for all
(k,\ell)\in[N]^{2}
,

\beta^{\mathsf{GT}}_{s}(k,\ell):=\mathbbm{1}_{\ell=A^{k}_{s-1}},\quad\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{s}(k,\ell):=\frac{\omega^{\ell}_{s-1}m_{s}(\xi^{\ell}_{s-1},\xi^{k}_{s})}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\omega^{j}_{s-1}m_{s}(\xi^{j}_{s-1},\xi^{k}_{s})}=\beta^{N}_{s}(\xi^{k}_{s},\xi^{\ell}_{s-1}),

where here again
\mathsf{GT}
stands for
genealogy tracing
and
\mathsf{BS}
for
backward sampling
. When the conditional distribution given
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}
is

\Lambda_{1,t}^{\mathsf{BS}}\otimes\Lambda_{2,t}^{\mathsf{BS}}
(resp.
\Lambda_{1,t}^{\mathsf{GT}}\otimes\Lambda_{2,t}^{\mathsf{GT}}
) we write
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{BS}}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}]
(resp.
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{GT}}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}]
). Define also for any
b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}
the coalescence function:

\mathrm{I}_{b,s}:([N]^{s+1})^{2}\ni(k^{1}_{0:s},k^{2}_{0:s})\mapsto\prod_{\ell=0}^{s}\{\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{\ell}=k^{2}_{\ell}}\mathbbm{1}_{b_{\ell}=1}+\mathbbm{1}_{k^{1}_{\ell}\neq k^{2}_{\ell}}\mathbbm{1}_{b_{\ell}=0}\},\quad\forall s\in[0:t],

(4.15)

for any
h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{2(t+1)}})
the random variable

\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)\!:=\!\prod_{s=0}^{t}N^{b_{s}}\bigg(\frac{N}{N-1}\bigg)^{1-b_{s}}\!\!\!\gamma_{t}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{BS}}\big[\mathrm{I}_{b,t}(K^{1}_{0:t},K^{2}_{0:t})h(\xi^{K^{1}_{0:t}}_{0:t},\xi^{K^{2}_{0:t}}_{0:t})|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\big],

(4.16)

and denote by
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{GT}}_{b,t}
the counterpart where the expectation in the r.h.s. is
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{GT}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

By (
4.15
), the random variable
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)
remains defined for any
b\in\mathcal{B}_{r}
with
r>t
and
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)=\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b_{0:t},t}(h)
where
b_{0:t}
is the truncation of
b
to the
t+1
first terms.

Finally, define for any
h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X})
, using
h_{t}:x_{0:t}\mapsto h(x_{t})
,

\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\gamma,t}(h):=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\big\{\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})-\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})\big\}.

(4.17)

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
b\in B_{t}
and any
h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{2(t+1)}})
,

(i)

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t-1}\big]=\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t-1}\big(g^{\otimes 2}_{t-1}\mathcal{M}^{b_{t}}_{t}[h]\big)
for all
t\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

(ii)

\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)
is an unbiased estimator of
\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}(h).

(iii)

If
h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X})
,
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\gamma,t}(h)
is an unbiased estimator of
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\gamma,t}(h)
.

By convention, we used in (i) the following notation:

g^{\otimes 2}_{t-1}\mathcal{M}^{b_{t}}_{t}[h]:(x_{0:t-1},x^{\prime}_{0:t-1})\mapsto g^{\otimes 2}_{t-1}(x_{t-1},x^{\prime}_{t-1})\int h(x_{0:t},x^{\prime}_{0:t})\mathcal{M}^{b_{t}}_{t}(x_{t-1},x^{\prime}_{t-1};\mathrm{d}x_{t},\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}_{t}).

The proof is provided in Section
A.2
of the appendix. First, (ii)
is a generalization of
[
28
, Lemma 2]
which states that
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{GT}}_{b,t}(h)
is also an unbiased estimator
\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}(h)
.
Its proof, see
[
28
, Supplementary]
, is based on a doubly conditional SMC argument
[
2
]
and while this scheme can be replicated to our estimator based on backward weights, we rather propose an alternative and elementary proof that also extends straightforwardly to
\mathsf{GT}
and which is based on our previous discussion. From (
4.4
), (iii) is a direct consequence of (ii) and provides an estimator of (
4.4
) based on a single particle run.

Theorem
4.4
deals with the convergence of
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)
for bounded
h
. The convergence in
\mathbf{L}_{2}
is stated under
(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\ref {assp:B}:\ref {assp:boundup}})
which are standard and the
1/\sqrt{N}
convergence rate is obtained under the additional assumption
(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:boundbelow})}}
. The equivalent result for
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{GT}}_{b,t}
is stated in
[
28
]
and is proved under
(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:B})}}
alone. From a technical point of view, this is possible because the use of indicators instead of backward weights allows for cancellations that simplify the analysis significantly. Assumption
(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:boundup})}}
enables us to show that the additional terms that come with the use of backward weights go to zero.

(
A3
)

For all
t>0
and
(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathsf{X}^{2}
,
m_{t}(x^{\prime},x)>0
.

(
A4
)

There exists
\sigma_{+}>0
such that for all
t\geq 1
,

\sup_{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}}m_{t}(x^{\prime},x)\leq\sigma_{+}
.

(
A5
)

There exists
0<\sigma_{-}<\sigma_{+}

such that for all
t\geq 1
,
\inf_{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}}m_{t}(x^{\prime},x)\geq\sigma_{-}
.

Assumption
(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:boundbelow})}}
is a strong assumption that is typically verified in models where the state space
\mathsf{X}
is compact. This assumption, together with
(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:boundup})}},
are now classic and have been widely used to obtain quantitative bounds in the SMC literature
[
19
,
15
,
27
]
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Assume that
(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\ref {assp:B}:\ref {assp:boundup}})
hold. For any
t\in\mathbb{N},

b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}
and
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{2(t+1)}})
,

\underset{N\to\infty}{\lim}\|\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)-\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}(h)\|_{2}=0.

(4.18)

In addition, if
(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:boundbelow})}}
holds the convergence rate is
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{N})
.

Remark 4.5
.

The dependence on the time horizon
t
of the
\mathbf{L}_{2}
bound is difficult to analyze and we did not undertake it in the proof. Adapting the proofs of the existing analysis
[
12
,
19
]
is not trivial as our smoothing estimators are non standard. Furthermore, the time dependence of the
\mathsf{GT}
counterpart has not been analyzed neither, which renders the comparison with our approach even more difficult.

The proof can be found in Section
A.4
of the appendix. As a straightforward consequence, the term by term estimator (
4.17
) of the asymptotic variance is weakly consistent.
It remains to detail how it can be computed. The next section is devoted to the exact computation of the estimators
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h)
and
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(h)
that appear in its expression.

4.2.
Computation for
b=0
and
b=e_{s}

We now derive practical expressions of
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h)
and
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(h)
in the practical case where

h:x_{0:t},x^{\prime}_{0:t}\mapsto h(x_{t},x^{\prime}_{t})\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{2(t+1)}})
. Define, for any
b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}
and any
t\geq 0
,

\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t}(K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t}):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{BS}}\big[\mathrm{I}_{b,t}(K^{1}_{0:t},K^{2}_{0:t})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t},K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t}\big].

(4.19)

Then, by the tower property,
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)
in (
4.16
) can be rewritten as

\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)=\prod_{s=0}^{t}N^{b_{s}}\bigg(\frac{N}{N-1}\bigg)^{1-b_{s}}\frac{\gamma_{t}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{k,\ell\in[N]^{2}}\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t}(k,\ell)h(\xi^{k}_{t},\xi^{\ell}_{t}).

(4.20)

Next, define for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
(k,\ell)\in[N]^{2}
,

S_{t}(k,\ell):=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}(k,\ell).

(4.21)

Plugging (
4.20
) in (
4.17
),
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\gamma,t}(h)
can be rewritten as

\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\gamma,t}(h)=\frac{N^{t-1}\gamma_{t}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}}{(N-1)^{t}}\sum_{k,\ell\in[N]^{2}}\left\{S_{t}(k,\ell)-\frac{t+1}{N-1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(k,\ell)\right\}h(\xi^{k}_{t})h(\xi^{\ell}_{t}).

(4.22)

The sequential computation
of
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\gamma,t}(h)

relies on that of
S_{t}(k,\ell)
in (
4.21
), and so
on that of
\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}(k,\ell)

and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(k,\ell)
.
By the tower property, we obtain the following recursions for
\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t}
:

\begin{cases}\mathcal{T}^{b}_{0}(k,\ell)=\mathbbm{1}_{k\neq\ell,b_{0}=0}+\mathbbm{1}_{k=\ell,b_{0}=1},\\
\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t}(k,\ell)=\mathbbm{1}_{k\neq\ell}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(k,i)\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(\ell,j)\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t-1}(i,j)&\mathrm{if}\quad b_{t}=0,\\
\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t}(k,\ell)=\mathbbm{1}_{k=\ell}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(k,i)\mathcal{W}^{j}_{t-1}\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t-1}(i,j)&\mathrm{if}\quad b_{t}=1,\end{cases}

(4.23)

for all
(k,\ell)\in[N]^{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. In particular, if
b=\mathbf{0}
,

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(k,\ell)=\mathbbm{1}_{k\neq\ell}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(k,i)\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(\ell,j)\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t-1}(i,j),

(4.24)

and if
b=e_{s}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}(k,\ell)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathbbm{1}_{k\neq\ell}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(k,i)\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(\ell,j)\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t-1}(i,j)&\quad t>s,\\
\mathbbm{1}_{k=\ell}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(k,i)\mathcal{W}^{j}_{t-1}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t-1}(i,j)&\quad t=s,\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(k,\ell)&\quad t<s.\\
\end{cases}

(4.25)

Next, combining (
4.21
)-(
4.25
) we obtain the online update of
S_{t}
:

S_{t}(k,\ell)=\mathcal{T}^{e_{t}}_{t}(k,\ell)+\mathbbm{1}_{k\neq\ell}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(k,i)\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(\ell,j)S_{t-1}(i,j),

(4.26)

for any
(k,\ell)\in[N]^{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
. We have shown that despite the sum over
s
that appears in (
4.17
) we are still able to update (
4.22
) at a computational cost independent of the time horizon
t
by propagating
S_{t}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}
.
Note that the algorithm provided in
[
28
, Algorithm 3, Supplementary]
does not compute
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{GT}}_{\gamma,t}
sequentially since it relies on the computation of each
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{GT}}_{e_{s},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})
and
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{GT}}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})
from scratch whenever a new observation is available. In Section
B.3
of the supplementary material we show how it can be computed online using the same ideas behind the previous derivations.

The computation of the estimates
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h)
and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\gamma,t}(h)
can benefit from parallelization by implementing the updates (
4.24
)-(
4.25
) with matrix operations:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t}=\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t-1}\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}\top}_{t}-\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t-1}\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}\top}_{t})&\quad\mathrm{if}\quad b_{t}=0,\\
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t}=\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t-1}\mathcal{W}^{1:N}_{t-1})&\quad\mathrm{if}\quad b_{t}=1.\end{cases}

4.3.
Variance estimators with reduced computational cost

In this section we derive a second estimator that relies only on the update of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}
.
Let
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X})
. By (
3.6
),
\sqrt{N}\big(\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)-\gamma_{t}(h)\big)
converges in distribution;
moreover,
N\big(\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)-\gamma_{t}(h)\big)^{2}
is uniformly integrable, using for instance a Hoeffding type inequality (see
[
16
]
). Hence
N\mathbb{E}[(\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)-\gamma_{t}(h))^{2}]
converges to the asymptotic variance
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\gamma,t}(h)
. On the other hand, using the lack of bias of
\gamma_{t}^{N}(h),

N\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)-\gamma_{t}(h)\right)^{2}\right]=N\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)^{2}\right]-\gamma_{t}(h)^{2}\right)=N\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)^{2}\right]-\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})\right).

A natural estimator of this quantity
is obtained by replacing both terms by their unbiased estimators

\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)^{2}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h_{t}^{\otimes 2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\gamma,t}(h)

\displaystyle:=N\big(\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)^{2}-\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h^{\otimes 2}_{t})\big)

(4.27)

\displaystyle=N\gamma_{t}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}\bigg(\eta_{t}^{N}(h)^{2}-\frac{N^{t-1}}{(N-1)^{t+1}}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(i,j)h(\xi^{i}_{t})h(\xi^{j}_{t})\bigg).

For the sake of completeness we also provide the estimator for the predictor and filter and defer their justification to the Section
B.2
of the supplementary material,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\eta,t}(h)

\displaystyle:=\frac{-N^{t}}{(N-1)^{t+1}}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(i,j)\big\{h(\xi^{i}_{t})-\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\big\}\big\{h(\xi^{j}_{t})-\eta_{t}^{N}(h)\big\},

(4.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\phi,t}(h)

\displaystyle:=\frac{-N^{t+2}}{(N-1)^{t+1}}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathcal{W}^{i}_{t}\mathcal{W}^{j}_{t}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(i,j)\big\{h(\xi^{i}_{t})-\phi_{t}^{N}(h)\big\}\big\{h(\xi^{j}_{t})-\phi_{t}^{N}(h)\big\}.

(4.29)

Remark 4.6
.

It is worthwhile to note the parallel between (
4.28
) and (
3.11
) (up to a negligible term depending on
N
); the indicator is replaced by the backward statistic
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(i,j)
which is the conditional probability of having two disjoint backward trajectories starting from
\xi^{i}_{t}
and
\xi^{j}_{t}
.

The convergence of (
4.27
) stated in Theorem
4.7
stems from the following identity which also appears in
[
28
,
18
]
and dates back to
[
8
]
:

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}}\bigg\{\prod_{s=0}^{t}\frac{1}{N^{b_{s}}}\bigg(\frac{N-1}{N}\bigg)^{1-b_{s}}\bigg\}\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}(h^{\otimes 2}_{t})

(4.30)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{t}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{BS}}\left[\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}}\mathrm{I}_{b,t}(K^{1}_{0:t},K^{2}_{0:t})h(\xi^{K^{1}_{t}}_{t})h(\xi^{K^{2}_{t}}_{t})\middle|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right]=\gamma_{t}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}\eta_{t}^{N}(h)^{2}=\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)^{2}.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\ref {assp:B}:\ref {assp:boundup}})
hold. For any
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X})
,
\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\gamma,t}(h)
converges in probability to
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\gamma,t}(h)
.

The proof is in Section
A.5
of the appendix. The main advantage of (
4.27
) w.r.t.
(
4.22
) is the computational cost. Indeed, remark that (
4.27
) only relies
on the sequential update of

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}
, contrary to
(
4.22
) which also relies
on that of
\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}
. Consequently,
the computational time of (
4.27
)
is roughly twice lower. In addition,
experiments show that the difference in performance is negligible so (
4.27
) is to be preferred in practice.

Remark 4.8
.

This alternative estimator does not invalidate the relevance of (
4.17
).
Indeed, remember that (
4.17
) is an unbiased estimator. Moreover, the asymptotic variance estimator of the FFBS algorithm that we provide in Section
5.2
is a term by term estimator that can be updated online in a way similar to (
4.17
).

4.4.
A PaRIS variance estimator

Let us discuss how the computational cost of (
4.27
) and (
4.22
)
can be further reduced
à la PaRIS

[
33
,
21
]
. In
[
33
]
, the forward only implementation of the FFBS algorithm is sped up by replacing the backward statistics by a conditionally unbiased estimator obtained by sampling particle indices according to the backward probabilities
\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}
through rejection sampling. We therefore apply the same idea here by letting
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{0}:=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{0}
and replacing
\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t}
with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t}(k,\ell)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{k\neq\ell}}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t-1}(J^{i}_{k,t-1},J^{i}_{\ell,t-1})

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{if}\quad b_{t}=0,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t}(k,\ell)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{k=\ell}}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathcal{W}^{j}_{t-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t-1}(J^{i}_{k,t-1},j)

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{if}\quad b_{t}=1,

where for any
k\in[N]
,
J^{1:M}_{k,t-1}

are i.i.d. samples according
to
\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(k,.)
. For
h\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{2}})
, the
PaRIS
estimator of
\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}(h)
is, for any
b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{N,M}_{b,t}(h)=\left\{\prod_{s=0}^{t}N^{b_{s}}\left(\frac{N}{N-1}\right)^{1-b_{s}}\right\}\frac{\gamma_{t}^{N}(1)^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{b}_{t}(i,j)h(\xi^{i}_{t},\xi^{j}_{t}),

(4.31)

and the
PaRIS
variance estimators are

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{V}}^{N,M}_{\gamma,t}(h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\big\{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{N,M}_{e_{s},t}(h^{\otimes 2})-\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{N,M}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h^{\otimes 2})\big\},

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\gamma,t}(h)

\displaystyle=N\big(\gamma_{t}^{N}(h)^{2}-\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{N,M}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h^{\otimes 2})\big),

(4.33)

where
M>1
refers to the number of sampled indices. The computation of (
4.27
) and (
4.33
) is summarized in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Update at step
t+1
of the variance estimators (
4.27
) and (
4.33
) associated to
\gamma_{t+1}^{N}(h)

M,\omega^{1:N}_{t},\xi^{1:N}_{t},\xi^{1:N}_{t+1}
,
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}
and
\gamma_{t}^{N}(\mathbf{1})

Compute
\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}

if

PaRIS

then

for

k\in[1:N]

do

Sample
J^{1:M}_{k,t}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}(k,.)

end

for

for

(k,\ell)\in[1:N]^{2}

do

Set
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}(k,\ell)=\mathbbm{1}_{k\neq\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(J^{i}_{k,t},J^{i}_{\ell,t})/M

end

for

else

Compute
\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}=\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}\prime}_{t+1}
.

Set
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}=\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}-\mathrm{Diag}(\overline{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1})
.

end

if

Compute
\bm{\mathcal{Q}}=\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}\odot\big[h(\xi^{1:N}_{t+1})h(\xi^{1:N}_{t+1})^{\top}\big]
.
\triangleright

h
is applied elementwise

return

N\gamma_{t+1}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}\big\{\eta^{N}_{t+1}(h)^{2}-N^{t}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\bm{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,j}/(N-1)^{t+2}\big\},\quad\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}
.

We are able to reduce the time complexity of computing
\mathcal{T}^{b}_{t}
to
\mathcal{O}(MN^{2})
. The key feature of the
PaRIS
approach is that
M
does not necessarily need to be large (see
[
33
, Section 3.1]
for a discussion on this matter). We impose
M>1
because then in the case
b=\mathbf{0}
, which is the case we are the most interested in, the support of
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\mathbf{0},t}(h)
is made of
N^{2}M^{t+1}
terms whereas when
M=1
it is only
N^{2}
. We show empirically in our experiments that setting
M=3
is sufficient to provide good results for the asymptotic variance estimation.

While it is not needed to obtain a
\mathcal{O}(MN^{2})
time complexity, the indices
J^{1:M}_{k,t-1}
can be sampled using an accept-reject procedure with the weights
\mathcal{W}^{1:N}_{t}
as proposals if the transition densities
m_{t}
are upper bounded. This approach does not require the computation of the normalizing constant of the backward weights (
4.6
). The computational time is then random but if the transition kernels are strongly mixing it can be provably further reduced
[
15
]
.
Theorem
4.9
is concerned with the convergence of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{N,M}_{b,t}(h)
for any bounded
h
and for any
fixed

M>1
. Its proof bears some similarity with that of Theorem
4.4
with the exception that the additional sampling introduces non trivial terms that need to be handled carefully. As a straightforward consequence, we obtain the convergence in probability of
\overline{\mathsf{V}}^{N,M}_{\gamma,t}(h)
for any
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X})
. The weak consistency of (
4.33
) in Theorem
4.10
is however less straightforward than that of Theorem
4.7
and relies on the insight that the identity (
4.30
) still holds when
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}
are replaced with their
PaRIS
versions. The proofs are provided respectively in Section
A.6
and
A.7
of the appendix.

Theorem 4.9
.

Assume that
(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\ref {assp:B}:\ref {assp:boundup}})
hold. For any
t\in\mathbb{N},

b\in\mathcal{B}_{t}
,
M>1
and
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{2}})
,

\underset{N\to\infty}{\lim}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{N,M}_{b,t}(h)-\mathcal{Q}_{b,t}(h)\|_{2}=0.

(4.34)

In addition, if
(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:boundbelow})}}
holds the convergence rate is
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{N})
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\ref {assp:B}:\ref {assp:boundup}})
hold. For all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
M>1
and
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X})
,
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\gamma,t}(h)
converges in probability to
\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{\gamma,t}(h)
when
N
goes to infinity.

5.
Application to the FFBS

In this section, we derive an estimator for the asymptotic variance of the
Forward Filtering Backward Smoothing
algorithm. We start by giving
a short presentation of the FFBS algorithm and we next derive an estimator of the asymptotic variance for additive functionals.

5.1.
FFBS algorithm

The FFBS algorithm aims at solving the well known degeneracy problem associated with the particle filter of Section
3.2
when it is used for approximating smoothing distributions. It relies on the following backward decomposition of the joint smoothing distribution:

\phi_{0:t|t}(h)=\int h(x_{0:t})\phi_{t}(\mathrm{d}x_{t})\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}(x_{t},\mathrm{d}x_{0:t-1}),

(5.1)

where
\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}
is the backward transition kernel from
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
to
(\mathsf{X}^{t},\mathcal{X}^{\otimes t})
:
\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{0}:=\text{ Id }
and for
t>0
,

\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}:=\mathrm{\mathbf{B}}_{\phi_{t-1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{\mathbf{B}}_{\phi_{0}}

and
\mathrm{\mathbf{B}}_{\phi_{s}}
is the backward kernel defined by

\mathrm{\mathbf{B}}_{\phi_{s}}(x_{s+1},A):=\frac{\int m_{s+1}(x_{s},x_{s+1})\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x_{s})\phi_{s}(\mathrm{d}x_{s})}{\phi_{s}(m_{s+1}(.,x_{s+1}))},\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{X},\forall x_{s+1}\in\mathsf{X}.

Denote by
\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}^{N}
the particle approximation of
\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}
where each backward kernel
\mathrm{\mathbf{B}}_{\phi_{s}}
is replaced by plugging in the particle approximation of the filter. This yields for any
A\in\mathcal{X}
and
x_{s+1}\in\mathsf{X}
,

\mathrm{\mathbf{B}}_{\phi_{s}}^{N}(x_{s+1},A):=\frac{\int m_{s+1}(x_{s},x_{s+1})\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x_{s})\phi_{s}^{N}(\mathrm{d}x_{s})}{\phi_{s}^{N}(m_{s+1}(.,x_{s+1}))}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\omega^{i}_{s}m_{s+1}(\xi^{i}_{s},x_{s+1})}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\omega^{j}_{s}m_{s+1}(\xi^{j}_{s},x_{s+1})}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(\xi^{i}_{s}).

Plugging this approximation and that of the filtering distribution in (
5.1
) yields

\phi^{N,\mathrm{FFBS}}_{0:t|t}(h):=\sum_{i_{0}=1}^{N}\cdots\sum_{i_{t}=1}^{N}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t}(i_{0:t})h(\xi^{i_{0}}_{0},\cdots,\xi^{i_{t}}_{t}),

(5.2)

where
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t}(i_{0:t}):=\mathcal{W}^{i_{t}}_{t}\prod_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t}(i_{s},i_{s-1})
. In the following, we write
\phi_{0:t|t}^{N}
for
\phi^{N,\mathrm{FFBS}}_{0:t|t}
and if
h
is such that
h:x_{0:t}\mapsto h(x_{s:\ell})
with
0\leq s\leq\ell\leq t
, we will instead write
\phi_{s:\ell|t}^{N}(h)
.

The theoretical properties of the FFBS are well understood in both the asymptotic regimes of
N
and
t

[
15
,
10
,
11
,
19
,
33
,
16
]
.
In particular, a Central Limit Theorem with an explicit expression of the asymptotic variance is established for any
h\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{t+1}})
under
(\mathbf{A}{\mathbf{\ref {assp:B})}}
in
[
15
, Theorem 8]
,

\sqrt{N}\big(\phi^{N}_{0:t|t}(h)-\phi_{0:t|t}(h)\big)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{N}\big(0,\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{FFBS}}_{0:t|t}(h)\big),

(5.3)

where

\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{FFBS}}_{0:t|t}(h):=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\frac{\eta_{s}\big(\mathrm{\mathbf{G}}_{s,t}\big[g_{t}\big\{h-\phi_{0:t|t}(h)\big\}\big]^{2})}{\eta_{s}(\overline{\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s+1:t}[g_{t}])^{2}}

(5.4)

and
\mathrm{\mathbf{G}}_{s,t}
is the kernel that integrates
h
forward and backward starting from
x_{s}
, i.e.

\mathrm{\mathbf{G}}_{s,t}[h](x_{s}):=\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{s+1:t}[h]\big](x_{s})=\int h(x_{0:t})\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}(x_{s},\mathrm{d}x_{0:s-1})\mathrm{\mathbf{Q}}_{s+1:t}(x_{s},\mathrm{d}x_{s+1:t}),

for any
s\in[0:t]
and
x_{s}\in\mathsf{X}
.

Unlike the asymptotic variance of filtering algorithms, no estimator of (
5.4
) exists in the literature, even though the FFBS and its variants are of significant importance in marginal smoothing and parameter estimation in HMMs
[
24
]
.
In this section, we bridge this gap by providing an online estimator for additive functionals
h
of the form

h_{0:t}(x_{0:t})=\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\tilde{h}_{s}(x_{s},x_{s+1}),

(5.5)

where for
s\in[0:t-1]
, we assume that
\tilde{h}_{s}
is bounded. For such functionals, the FFBS can be computed online with a
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
time complexity per time step, i.e. whenever a new observation is processed.
For
0\leq s<r\leq t
, we write
\tilde{h}_{s:r}(x_{s:r})=\sum_{\ell=s}^{r-1}\tilde{h}_{\ell}(x_{\ell},x_{\ell+1})
.
Expectations of functionals of the form (
5.5
) include marginal smoothing, pairwise marginal smoothing and the
E
-step of the Expectation Maximization algorithm.

Before we derive our estimator, let us first recall why the FFBS can be indeed computed online in this case. For more details on the forward only implementation of the FFBS and its variants we refer the reader to
[
16
,
33
]
. For any
t>0
and any additive functional
h_{0:t}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}[h_{0:t}](x_{t})

\displaystyle=\int\left\{\tilde{h}_{0:t-1}(x_{0:t-1})+\tilde{h}_{t-1}(x_{t-1},x_{t})\right\}\mathrm{\mathbf{B}}_{\phi_{t-1}}(x_{t},\mathrm{d}x_{t-1})\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t-1}(x_{t-1},\mathrm{d}x_{0:t-2})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathbf{B}}_{\phi_{t-1}}\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t-1}[\tilde{h}_{0:t-1}]+\tilde{h}_{t-1}(.,x_{t})\big](x_{t}).

Then, plugging in the particle approximations, we obtain the following recursion

\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}^{N}[h_{0:t}](x_{t})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\omega^{i}_{t-1}m_{t}(\xi^{i}_{t-1},x_{t})}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\omega^{j}_{t-1}m_{t}(\xi^{j}_{t-1},x_{t})}\big\{\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t-1}^{N}[\tilde{h}_{0:t-1}](\xi^{i}_{t-1})+\tilde{h}_{t-1}(\xi^{i}_{t-1},x_{t})\big\},

(5.6)

and then
\phi^{N}_{0:t|t}(h_{0:t})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{W}^{i}_{t}\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}^{N}[h_{0:t}](\xi^{i}_{t})
.
Therefore,
\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t}^{N}[h_{0:t}]
needs only to be estimated at the particle locations and smoothing estimates for additive functionals can be computed with the forward pass and has
\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
complexity per time step.

5.2.
Asymptotic variance estimator

From now on we will assume that
h_{0:t}

satisfies (
5.5
). Our estimator is based on the following alternative
expression of the asymptotic variance (
5.4
)

\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{FFBS}}_{0:t|t}(h)=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\frac{\gamma_{s}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{s}\big(\mathrm{\mathbf{G}}_{s,t}[g_{t}\{h_{0:t}-\phi_{0:t|t}(h_{0:t})\}]^{2})}{\gamma_{t+1}(\mathbf{1})^{2}},

(5.7)

which is deduced
using the definitions given in Section
3
. This expression is motivated by Proposition
5.1
in which we express the numerators that appear in (
5.7
) in terms of expectations with respect to
\mathcal{Q}_{e_{s},t}
. The proof is given in Section
A.3
of the appendix.

Proposition 5.1
.

For any
s\in[0:t]
and any additive functional
h_{0:t}\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{t+1}})
,

\gamma_{s}(\mathbf{1})\gamma_{s}\big(\mathrm{\mathbf{G}}_{s,t}[h_{0:t}]^{2})=\mathcal{Q}_{e_{s},t}\big(\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[\tilde{h}_{0:s}]+\tilde{h}_{s:t}\big]^{\otimes 2}\big).

(5.8)

By Theorem
4.4
, for any additive functional
h_{0:t}
as in (
5.5
), we have that
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}([\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[\tilde{h}_{0:s}]+\tilde{h}_{s:t}]^{\otimes 2})
is a consistent estimator
of
\mathcal{Q}_{e_{s},t}([\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[\tilde{h}_{0:s}]+\tilde{h}_{s:t}]^{\otimes 2})
, but

\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[h_{0:s}]
is intractable and we only have access to its particle approximation
\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{0:s}]
. Our
proposed estimator of the asymptotic variance (
5.3
) is then

\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{0:t|t}(h_{t}):=\sum_{s=0}^{T}\frac{\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}\big(\big[g_{t}\{\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[\tilde{h}_{0:s}]+\tilde{h}_{s:t}-\phi_{0:t|t}^{N}(h_{0:t})\}\big]^{\otimes 2}\big)}{\gamma_{t+1}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}},

(5.9)

where we have replaced
\phi_{0:t|t}(h_{t})
by its FFBS estimator. Remark that Theorem
4.4
cannot be applied to
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}\big(\big[g_{t}\{\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[\tilde{h}_{0:s}]+\tilde{h}_{s:t}\big]^{\otimes 2}\big)
because its proof relies on the fact that the function
h
integrated by
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}
does not depend on the particles.

Theorem
5.2
proved in Section
A.8
of the supplementary material shows that weak consistency still holds under the assumptions of Theorem
4.4
. The proof proceeds in three steps. We first establish that for all
s>0
and additive functional
h_{0:s}
,
\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{0:s}](x_{s})
converges
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. to
\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[h_{0:s}](x_{s})
for any
x_{s}\in\mathsf{X}
. Then, we use it to show that at
t=s
, the distance in
\mathbf{L}_{2}
between
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},s}\big(\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{0:s}]c_{s}+\tilde{h}_{s}\big]\otimes\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[f_{0:s}]d_{s}+\tilde{f}_{s}\big]\big)
and the ”idealized” consistent estimator
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},s}\big(\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[h_{0:s}]c_{s}+\tilde{h}_{s}\big]\otimes\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[f_{0:s}]d_{s}+\tilde{f}_{s}\big]\big)
, goes to 0. Finally, we extend the result to
t>s
by induction, similarly to Theorem
4.4
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Assume that
(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\ref {assp:B}:\ref {assp:boundup}})
hold. For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\in[0:t]
,
(\tilde{h}_{s:t},\tilde{f}_{s:t})\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X}^{\otimes{t-s+1}})^{2}
,
(c_{t},d_{t})\in\mathbb{F}_{b}(\mathcal{X})^{2}
and additive functionnals
(h_{0:s},f_{0:s})
(
5.5
),

\underset{N\to\infty}{\lim}\big\|\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}\big(\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{0:s}]c_{t}+\tilde{h}_{s:t}\big]\otimes\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[f_{0:s}]d_{t}+\tilde{f}_{s:t}\big]\big)\\
-\mathcal{Q}_{e_{s},t}\big(\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[h_{0:s}]c_{t}+\tilde{h}_{s:t}\big]\otimes\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}[f_{0:s}]d_{t}+\tilde{f}_{s:t}\big]\big)\big\|_{2}=0,

(5.10)

and for any additive functional (
5.5
),
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{0:t|t}(h_{0:t})
converges in probability to
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{FFBS}}_{0:t|t}(h_{0:t})
.

5.3.
Algorithm for marginal smoothing

We now provide an algorithm for the case
h_{\ell}:x_{0:t}\mapsto h_{\ell}(x_{\ell})
known
as the marginal smoothing problem. For such functions (
5.9
) is defined for
t\geq\ell
and simplifies to

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\ell|t}(h_{\ell}):=\frac{1}{\gamma_{t+1}^{N}(\mathbf{1})^{2}}\bigg\{\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}\big(\big[g_{t}\{h_{\ell}-\phi_{\ell|t}^{N}(h_{\ell})\}\big]^{\otimes 2}\big)

(5.11)

\displaystyle+\sum_{s=\ell+1}^{t}\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}\big(\big[g_{t}\{\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{\ell}]-\phi_{\ell|t}^{N}(h_{\ell})\}\big]^{\otimes 2}\big)\bigg\}.

When
\ell=t
we recover the term by term estimator of the filter which is consistent with the fact that
\phi_{t|t}^{N}(h)=\phi_{t}^{N}(h)
. Using the bilinearity of
\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{b,t}
yields

\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{\ell|t}(h_{\ell})=R^{\ell}_{1,t}-\phi_{\ell|t}^{N}(h_{\ell})R^{\ell}_{2,t}+\phi_{\ell|t}^{N}(h_{\ell})^{2}R_{t},

(5.12)

where
R_{t}:=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(g_{t}^{\otimes 2})
and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}R^{1}_{\ell,t}&:=\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}\big([g_{t}h_{\ell}]^{\otimes 2}\big)+\sum_{s=\ell+1}^{t}\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(\big[g_{t}\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{\ell}]\big]^{\otimes 2}),\\
R^{2}_{\ell,t}&:=\big\{\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(g_{t}h_{\ell}\otimes g_{t})+\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(g_{t}\otimes g_{t}h_{\ell})\big\}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+\big\{\sum_{s=\ell+1}^{t}\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(g_{t}\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{\ell}]\otimes g_{t})+\mathcal{Q}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{e_{s},t}(g_{t}\otimes g_{t}\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{\ell}])\big\}.\end{cases}

Mirroring (
4.25
), define for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\in[0:t]
and
f_{n}:x_{0:t},x^{\prime}_{0:t}\mapsto f(x_{n},x^{\prime}_{n})
the random variable

\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}[f_{n}](K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t}):=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{I}_{e_{s},t}(K^{1}_{0:t},K^{2}_{0:t})f_{n}(\xi^{K^{1}_{n}}_{n},\xi^{K^{2}_{n}}_{n})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t-1},K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t}\big]

(5.13)

and also write
S^{1}_{t,t}(K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t})=S_{t}(K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t})h^{\otimes 2}_{t}(\xi^{K^{1}_{t}}_{t},\xi^{K^{2}_{t}}_{t})
,
S^{2}_{t,t}(K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t})=S_{t}(K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t})h^{\oplus 2}_{t}(\xi^{K^{1}_{t}}_{t},\xi^{K^{2}_{t}}_{t})
and for any
t>\ell
,

\displaystyle S^{1}_{\ell,t}(K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}[h^{\otimes 2}_{\ell}](K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t})+\sum_{s=\ell+1}^{t}\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{\ell}]^{\otimes 2}\big](K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t}),

\displaystyle S^{2}_{\ell,t}(K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}[h^{\oplus 2}_{\ell}](K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t})+\sum_{s=\ell+1}^{t}\mathcal{T}^{e_{s}}_{t}\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{N}[h_{\ell}]^{\oplus 2}\big](K^{1}_{t},K^{2}_{t}),

where for any
f_{\ell}
,
f^{\oplus 2}_{\ell}:x_{\ell},x^{\prime}_{\ell}\mapsto f_{\ell}(x_{\ell})+f_{\ell}(x^{\prime}_{\ell})
and
S_{\ell}
is defined in (
4.21
). Applying the tower property we get that

\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{\ell|t}(h_{t})=N\left(\frac{N}{N-1}\right)^{t}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathcal{W}^{i}_{t}\mathcal{W}^{j}_{t}\big\{S^{1}_{\ell,t}(i,j)-\phi_{\ell|t}^{N}(h_{\ell})S^{2}_{\ell,t}(i,j)+\phi_{\ell|t}^{N}(h_{\ell})^{2}S_{t}(i,j)\big\}.

The quantities
S^{1}_{\ell,t+1}
,
S^{2}_{\ell,t+1}
may be updated online using the following recursions which are again obtained by applying the tower property

S^{1}_{\ell,t+1}(i,j):=\mathcal{T}^{e_{t+1}}_{t+1}(i,j)\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}^{N}[h_{\ell}](\xi^{i}_{t+1})\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}^{N}[h_{\ell}](\xi^{j}_{t+1})\\
+\mathbbm{1}_{i\neq j}\sum_{m,n\in[N]^{2}}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}(i,m)\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}(j,n)S^{1}_{\ell,t}(m,n),

(5.14)

and

S^{2}_{\ell,t+1}(i,j):=\mathcal{T}^{e_{t+1}}_{t+1}(i,j)\big\{\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}^{N}[h_{\ell}](\xi^{i}_{t+1})+\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}^{N}[h_{\ell}](\xi^{j}_{t+1})\big\}\\
+\mathbbm{1}_{i\neq j}\sum_{m,n\in[N]^{2}}\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}(i,m)\beta^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}(j,n)S^{2}_{\ell,t}(m,n).

(5.15)

The updates of

S^{1}_{\ell,t+1}(i,j)
and
of
S^{2}_{\ell,t+1}(i,j)
are thus similiar to that of
S_{t}
in (
4.26
). The computation
of the variance estimator is described in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 2

Update at step
t+1
of the variance estimator for marginal smoothing

\bm{\mathcal{W}}^{1:N}_{t+1},\bm{\mathcal{W}}^{1:N}_{t},\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1},\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}^{N}[h_{\ell}],\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t},\bm{S}^{1}_{\ell,t},\bm{S}^{2}_{\ell,t},\phi_{\ell|t+1}^{N}(h_{\ell})

Compute
\bm{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}^{e_{t+1}}_{t+1}=\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}\bm{\mathcal{W}}_{t}
,
\quad\bm{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}=\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}\top}_{t+1}
,
\quad\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{t+1}=\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}\bm{S}_{t}\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}\top}_{t+1}

Set
\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{e_{t+1}}_{t+1}=\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}^{e_{t+1}}_{t+1})
,
\quad\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}=\bm{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1}-\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1})
,
\quad\bm{S}_{t+1}=\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{t+1}-\mathrm{Diag}(\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{t+1})+\mathcal{T}^{e_{t+1}}_{t+1}

for
i
\in\{1,2\}

do

Compute
\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{i}_{\ell,t+1}=\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}}_{t+1}\bm{S}^{i}_{\ell,t}\bm{\beta}^{\mathsf{BS}\top}_{t+1}
.

Set
\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{i}_{\ell,t+1}=\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{i}_{t+1}-\mathrm{Diag}(\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{i}_{t+1})

end

for

Set
\bm{S}^{1}_{\ell,t+1}=\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{1}_{\ell,t+1}+\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{e_{t+1}}_{t+1}\odot\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}[h_{\ell}]\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}[h_{\ell}]^{\top}]

Set
\bm{S}^{2}_{\ell,t+1}=\bm{\widetilde{S}}^{2}_{\ell,t+1}+\bm{\mathcal{T}}^{e_{t+1}}_{t+1}\odot\big[\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}[h_{\ell}]+\mathrm{\mathbf{T}}_{t+1}[h_{\ell}]^{\top}]

Set
\overline{\bm{S}}_{\ell,t+1}=\bm{S}^{1}_{\ell,t+1}-\phi_{\ell|t+1}^{N}(h_{\ell})\bm{S}^{2}_{\ell,t+1}+\phi_{\ell|t+1}^{N}(h_{\ell})^{2}\bm{S}_{t+1}

return

-N^{t+2}/(N-1)^{t+1}\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathcal{W}^{i}_{t+1}\mathcal{W}^{j}_{t+1}\overline{S}_{\ell,t+1}(i,j),\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t+1},\bm{S}^{1}_{\ell,t+1},\bm{S}^{2}_{\ell,t+1},\bm{S}_{t+1}
.

6.
Numerical simulations

We now demonstrate our estimators on particle filtering and smoothing examples in HMMs (see Ex.
3.1
). We assume in this section that
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Y}=\mathbb{R}
and that the dominating measure is the Lebesgue measure. The model considered is the
stochastic volatility model
with, for all
n\geq 1
,

X_{n+1}=\varphi X_{n}+\sigma U_{n+1}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad Y_{n}=\beta\exp(X_{n}/2)V_{n},

(6.1)

with
(\varphi,\beta,\sigma)=(.975,.641,.165)
,

\{U_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{V_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

are two sequences of independent standard Gaussian noises and
U_{n}
is independent of
V_{m}
for all

(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
.
The state process
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is initialized with a Gaussian distribution with zero mean and variance
\sigma^{2}/(1-\varphi^{2})
. These are the exact values and initialization used in
[
32
]
.
The assumptions on the model under which
[
32
]
conduct their theoretical analysis and
(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\ref {assp:A}:\ref {assp:boundup}})
are satisfied for this model. All the simulations are run on GPU and the implementations using matrix operations are available at
https://github.com/yazidjanati/asymptoticvariance
.

6.1.
Asymptotic variance of the predictor

We are interested in the estimation of the asymptotic variance of the predictor
\eta_{t}^{N}(\rm Id)

at each time step

t
. The estimator is given in Section
B.2
of the appendix.
We use synthetic datasets sampled from (
6.1
). The real asymptotic variances are intractable and we estimate them by repeating independently and a thousand times the computation of each predictor mean
\eta_{t}^{N}(\rm Id)
with
N=10000
and then multiplying the sample variance by
N
.

We first investigate how the three variance estimators based on backward sampling
\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\eta,t}
,
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\eta,t}
and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\eta,t}
behave in terms of computational time, bias and variance. The
PaRIS
estimator is used with
M=3
without rejection sampling. For this first experiment, we sampled 750 observations from (
6.1
) and ran
50
particle filters with
N=3000
from which we obtained 50 replicates of each asymptotic variance estimate. The results are reported in Figure
1
.
The three estimators exhibit approximately the same variance but the term by term version becomes slightly more biased at
t
increases. Strikingly, the
PaRIS
estimator
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\eta,t}
behaves similarly to
\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\eta,t}
with a much lower computational time and complexity as can be seen on the left plot of Figure
6
in the supplementary material.

We now compare
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\eta,t}
with
M=3
to
[
4
,
28
,
32
]
on two different observation records of different length.
For the lag size parameter
\lambda
, we found that
\lambda=20
has the best bias-variance trade-off by comparing the obtained fixed lag estimates with the crude asymptotic variance estimator.
Note that in realistic situations choosing the right
\lambda
is non trivial (besides the strong mixing case, as argued in
[
32
]
) and for this reason we conducted the experiments with two additional lag values,
\lambda\in\{100,200\}
. For moderately long observation records
(t\in[750])
and with
N=3000
,
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\eta,t}
compares favorably in terms of bias-variance trade-off with the best lagged estimator and even has similar computational time on GPU. The results are reported in Figure
2
.
The five different estimators are computed with the same particle cloud and replicated 50 times. As expected, when the lag is increased the fixed lag estimates exhibit more variance because of the particle degeneracy. In the extreme case where the lag is set to
750
(CLE) bias and variance both increase significantly, as showcased in the fifth plot.

Figure 1.
Long-term behavior of
\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\eta,t}
(top),
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\eta,t}
with
M=3
(middle) and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\eta,t}
(bottom). The black dashed line is the asymptotic variance estimated using brute force. The number of particles is set to
N=2000
.

Figure 2.
Long-term behavior of the asymptotic variance estimators up to
t=750
. From top to bottom:
PaRIS
version of
\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\eta,t}
with
M=3
, lagged estimators with (in order)
\lambda\in\{20,100,200,750\}
. The case
\lambda=750
corresponds to the CLE estimator. For each estimator, the blurred colored lines represent each run out of fifty runs and solid colored lines correspond to their average. The black dashed line is the asymptotic variance obtained by brute force. The number of particles
N
is set to
2000
.

Figure 3.
Long-term behavior of the asymptotic variance estimates up to
t=3000
. White dots represent the average of the asymptotic variance estimates of each algorithm. The dashed black lines correspond to the asymptotic variances estimated by brute force.
N
is set to
5000
on the left boxplot and
10000
on the right one. The boxplots at each time step from left to right are:
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\eta,t}
with
M=3
and then the lagged CLEs with
\lambda\in\{20,100,200,3000\}
.

For the longer time horizon
t\in[3000]
we set
N=5000
and picked three time steps in order to monitor the bias and variance closely, see Figure
3
. The variance of
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\eta,t}
remains steady while the bias increases gradually but slowly. This is attributed to the fact that our estimator is a ratio of two estimators with increasing bias and variance. Nonetheless, our estimator remains competitive with the best fixed lag estimator. Doubling the number of particles decreases both bias and variance, as highlighted by the right plot. The computational cost of
\mathsf{V}^{N,M}_{\eta,t}
is approximately twice larger than that of the genealogy tracing estimators when
N=5000
as shown in Figure
6
in the supplementary material. However, we are able to maintain a small variance and bias without having to tune any other parameter besides the sample size. In more complicated models or realistic scenarios, we do not know in advance which lag size is suitable and using an inappropriate lag might yield poor estimates.
We further investigate the stability of our estimator with respect to
t
by comparing

D^{\mathsf{BS}}_{N}(t):=\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{0}}_{t}(i,j)/N(N-1),\quad D^{\mathsf{GT}}_{N}(t):=\sum_{i,j\in[N]^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{E}^{i}_{t,0}\neq\mathrm{E}^{j}_{t,0}}/N(N-1),

which are central in the expression of the variance estimators (see Remark
4.6
). We also compare
E^{\mathsf{BS}}_{N}(t):=\big|(\mathcal{V}^{N,\mathsf{BS}}_{\eta,t}(\rm Id)/\mathcal{V}^{\infty}_{t}(\rm Id))-1\big|
and
E^{\mathsf{GT}}_{N}(t)
which is defined in an analogous way.
For the CLE (
3.11
), although it is expected to collapse to
0
after
\mathcal{O}(N)
timesteps following
[
25
]
, the estimator starts to exhibit high bias and variance much before as the set of time
0
ancestors depletes at a fast rate. This is illustrated on the left plot of Figure
4
where we fix
N
to
1000
and vary
t
between
0
and
3000
. We see that
D^{\mathsf{GT}}_{N}(t)
decreases much faster than
D^{\mathsf{BS}}_{N}(t)
and this in turn translates into longer stability for our estimator as can be seen on the right plot of the same figure.

Figure 4.
Dependency on the time
t
of the variance estimators. The right plot displays the empirical error
E_{N}(t)
for both
\mathsf{BS}
and
\mathsf{GT}
with
N
fixed to
1000
. We display the median and the interquartile range over
30
runs. The left plot displays the median of
D_{N}(t)
associated with the
\mathsf{BS}
and
\mathsf{GT}
variance estimators used on the right plot.

6.2.
Asymptotic variance of the smoother

Here we are interested in the estimation of the asymptotic variance associated to the FFBS estimates of the marginal means
\phi_{\ell|t}(\mathrm{Id})
with
\ell
fixed and
t\geq\ell
varying using Algorithm
2
. For this example we sampled four different observation records of length
160
each and
\ell
is set to
100
.
The real asymptotic variances of each
\phi_{\ell|t}(\mathrm{Id})
are intractable and they are estimated using
1000
independent replicates of the marginal means
\phi_{\ell|t}^{N}(\mathrm{Id})
with
N=10000
. We then multiply the obtained sample variance by
N
. The results are reported in Figure
5
. As expected, the crude estimates of the asymptotic variances all stagnate after some time
t
